conference on 10 November 2026 after market hours. We believe AVC will 
be one of the key beneficiaries from the rising penetration rate of liquid 
cooling in both ASIC and GPU projects, with an aggressive capacity 
expansion plan over 2026-28. We reaffirm our Buy (1) call (see our latest 
Flash, 3Q26 results preview: likely to beat expectations, on 2 November).

What’s the impact: 3Q26 results beat the Bloomberg consensus. 
AVC’s 3Q26 revenue of TWD59,089m (+20.3% QoQ; +51.8% YoY) was 
roughly in line with our and the Bloomberg consensus estimates. Its 3Q26 
gross margin came in at 33.9% (vs. 32.6% in 2Q26 and 26.1% in 3Q25), a 
record-high level, higher than our estimate (33.5%) and the consensus 
(33.3%) on better product mix and high server revenue contribution of 66% 
in 3Q26 vs. 66% in 2Q26 and 54% in 3Q25. Its operating profit of 
TWD16,856m (+25.1% QoQ; +108.5% YoY) was in line with our estimate 
and beat the consensus by 4.2%. Thus, its operating margin of 28.5% (vs. 
27.4% in 2Q26 and 20.8% in 3Q25), a record-high level, was also in line 
with our estimate and beat the consensus (27.9%). With an FX loss of 
TWD304m, its net profit of TWD11,564m (+20.9% QoQ; +116.6% YoY) was 
in line with our estimate and beat the market consensus estimate by 3%. Its 
3Q26 EPS finished at TWD29.32.

Key highlights from the investor conference – liquid cooling revenue 
from AI servers continue to rise. AVC is confident it can maintain its 
leading position as a thermal and mechanical solution integrator, being a 
primary design partner for ASIC and GPU projects. In 2027, AVC expects 
its revenue from ASICs projects to be higher than GPU ones. From 3Q26, 
AVC has started shipping the double-sided cold plates, which it expects to 
be adopted by all ASIC clients and some GPU clients. With years of 
experience in providing CDU parts, AVC has also started shipping complete 
CDU solutions from 3Q26. AVC aims for TWD15-20bn capex in 2027 
(mainly in Vietnam, for liquid cooling capacity expansion), up from 
TWD15bn in 2026E. We keep our 2026-28E EPS broadly unchanged.

What we recommend: We reaffirm our Buy (1) call and 12M TP of 
TWD4,815, still based on a target PER of 30x vs. its past-3-year PER 
range of 5-25x [average PER of 15x]) applied to our 1-year-forward EPS. 
Downside risks: slower-than-expected progress in high-gross-margin 
orders, slow AI server adoption, and strong competition.

How we differ: Our 2028E EPS is 8% above consensus, likely on our 
more aggressive gross- and operating margins.

10 November 2026

Company profile 
AVC is a global leading comprehensive thermal solutions provider in segments related to desktops, 
laptops, communication, servers, energy and autos. Its main product line includes thermal products 
(thermal modules and fans), chassis, system assembly and peripheral products and hinges. The 
company has been listed on Taiwan’s main board since September 2002.

### Page 5

Asia Vital Components (3017 TT): 10 November 2026 
ESG analysis

Update Date: 6 May 2026 
Source: Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 2
The CEO and Chairperson of AVC are the same individual, but we see no issue with its board
quality. Besides, we see the Chairperson has 24 years of experience in this industry, which is a
positive sign from an executive perspective.
Capital management 2 AVC's dividend payout ratio was 40-52% over 2020-25, likely due to its capacity expansion plans.
Related party &
transaction 2 Revenue generated from related-party transactions was 1-3% of total revenue during 2021-25, and we found no suspicious activity.
E Supply chain management 1
AVC has introduced Hazardous Substance Process Management (HSPM) and received
QC080000 certification. In addition, AVC set up a Green Product team to promote HSPM and
formulate environmental regulation to make sure its material sourcing and efficiency are well-
performed.
E Data security 1
To prevent the interruption of information systems and leakage of sensitive information causing
losses to the company and customers, AVC has introduced an information security management
system and passed the ISO 27001 information security management system certification in
2019.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

3Q26Pre: 환율 하락에도 성장 흐름 유지 
매출액 1조 3,477억원(+7.2%YoY, 이하 YoY 생략), 영업이익 5,957억원(-6.0%, OPM 
44.2%)로 컨센서스에 부합할 것으로 전망. 3분기 원달러 평균 환율은 1,423원으로 전분기 
평균 환율 대비 약 5.2% 하락에도 불구하고 성장 흐름 지속. 
2분기 노조 파업으로 인한 영향은 3분기 생산을 진행했으며 4분기 매출에 반영될 예정으로 
연간 실적에 미치는 영향은 제한적일 것으로 예상. GSK로부터 인수한 록빌 공장도 안정적
으로 생산이 이루어지고 있으며 CAPA 여유분은 추가 수주를 진행 중으로 28~29년 이후 
완전 가동 전망. 
4Q, 6공장 건설과 폴리펩타이드 인수 
유상 증자 관련 투자 설명서 상 투자 계획에 따르면 6공장 투자는 26년부터 29년까지 진행 
될 예정으로 29년 가동 목표. 6공장은 5공장과 동일한 18만 리터 규모로 건설되며, 연내 투
자 집행을 시작함에 따라 건설 발표도 임박한 것으로 추정. 동사는 일정 수준 이상 기존 공
장 수주를 확보한 후 후속 공장 건설을 진행해오고 있어 non-binding 수주 계약의 본계약 
전환도 순차적으로 이루어질 것으로 전망. 
폴리펩타이드 인수도 현재 공개 매수를 진행 중에 있으며 연내 마무리될 것으로 예상. 
1H26 폴리펩타이드 매출은 2.37억 유로(+41.6%)로 GLP-1 agonist 등 펩타이드 의약품 
수요 증가에 따라 높은 성장 지속. 
투자 의견 Buy, 목표 주가 200만원으로 하향 
매수 의견을 유지하며 목표 주가는 기존 210만원에서 200만원으로 하향. 목표 주가 하향은 
유상 증자에 따른 EPS 희석에 기인. 글로벌 항체 CDMO 수요가 여전히 견조하며, 폴리펩
타이드 인수로 펩타이드로의 확장에 따른 추가적인 성장 기대. 주주 대상 유상 증자 영향과 
파업 우려 등이 있으나 노사 합의 시 주가 반등 전망.

## Document 269: AI is boosting business creations both in the US and Europe (2026-11-04)

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI is boosting business creations both in the US and Europe (2026-11-04).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-11-04
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

4 November 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
AI IS BOOSTING BUSINESS CREATIONS BOTH IN THE US AND EUROPE
Stéphane Colliac
The significant increase in business creations since mid-2025 in the United States, France and Germany – and, to a lesser extent, in the United
Kingdom – is continuing. Across the board, this trend is being driven by the information and communication sector and mostly by AI. It is
spreading to other business services, while developments in consumer services, industry and construction are more mixed. However, in both

the United States and France, the trend is broadening, suggesting that AI is facilitating business creation across all sectors.
Business creations by sector: a boom that is building momentum and spreading

We have calculated the year-on-year change in the number of new business start-ups. The table categorises these results as positive (red), neutral (white) or negative (blue). The darker
the colour, the larger the change. The data series are defined as follows: Germany = new entries in the companies register (start-ups) since 2008; France = business start-ups, including

micro-entrepreneurs, since 2000; United Kingdom = businesses registered for VAT/PAYE since 2017; United States = business applications (tax identification number) since 2004. Sectors
have been grouped together to ensure that the classification is standardised across all countries. For the third quarter, the German data only include the months of July and August.

### Page 2

2
The bank for
a changing
world
Chart of the Week / 4 November 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
A noticeable boom in the United States, France, and Germany, but not quite as pronounced in the United Kingdom
The four economies for which we have comparable data (the United States, France, Germany and the United Kingdom) have all
seen, to varying degrees, an increase in the number of new business creations. While the timing varies across these countries,

most of them are enjoying strong momentum. In the United States, there has been a noticeable acceleration since the second
quarter of 2025, which has continued to strengthen. Germany has followed a similar trajectory, moving from slight declines at
the start of 2024 to sharp increases. France is experiencing the same trend but with some specific features, as momentum has
picked up significantly after months of slowdown (due to the uncertainty following the dissolution of the National Assembly) and is

accelerating across most sectors in 2026. In the United Kingdom, the trend is less pronounced and the increase may not last.
Business services, and AI in particular, are driving this momentum
In most of these four countries, business creations are rising significantly in the information and communication sector, which is
directly linked to AI. In the United States, Germany and France, there has been a pronounced trend which is gaining momentum.
In the United Kingdom, the AI sector is also seeing an increase, but it is significantly less pronounced. However, this momentum is

not limited to this sector. It is spreading to many services sectors, including finance, real estate and business services. Growth has
been sustained since mid-2025 in the United States and Germany, and since the third quarter of 2025 in France.
The picture for consumer services is more mixed. In the United States, trade, accommodation and catering have experienced major
increases in creations since mid-2025. In France, these sectors are in positive territory, particularly trade, where the increase in

business creations has recently accelerated. In Germany, these same sectors fell into negative territory in 2026 following a rather
mixed year in 2025.
The situation is even more mixed for manufacturing and construction. In the United States, these sectors are also part of the
overall positive momentum in the third quarter of 2026. This shows that AI does not only benefit sectors closely linked to it
(notably the digital technology sector) but that it makes it easier to start a business across the entire economy (by simplifying

administrative procedures in particular). In France, the upturn in business creations in the construction sector suggests that this
same trend is at play, even as activity in the sector continues to contract.
Therefore, the trend is more widespread in the United States and is becoming increasingly prevalent in France. It is driven by
specific sectors of the services industry in Germany, while it is significantly more subdued in the United Kingdom.

forecasts are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The Cotality home value index, covering all of Australia, fell
1.2% in October with slight revisions to previous months
again shaving a further 0.3% off prices. The correction
has now seen prices decline 6.7% from their March peak

with annual growth firmly in negative at –2.8%yr. Turnover
continues fall as well, down 22.5%yr. Housing-related
tax policy changes, interest rate rises and heightened
uncertainty have triggered a sharp contraction in markets.
The downturn is now comparable to the previous two
housing market corrections. Nationally, the downturn in
2022-23 saw prices decline 7.5% and turnover fall 29% peak
to trough. The 2017-18 correction saw prices fall 8.6% and

turnover by 24%, with larger falls in Sydney and Melbourne.
Note that unless specified, figures in titles, tables and
commentary are in non‑seasonally adjusted terms, consistent
with the ‘headline’ figures reported by Cotality. However,
figures shown in charts are in seasonally adjusted terms.
Preliminary estimates of turnover show rolling 3mth volumes
down 5.6%qtr in October, previous estimates again revised
lower. The monthly pattern suggests declines re ‑accelerated

through September‑October after stabilising somewhat in July ‑
August. The RBA's interest rate rise in late September and a
further expected tightening likely contributed to the shift.
The detail shows Brisbane and Perth are now leading the
way in terms of monthly price declines although corrections
to date remain significantly larger in Sydney and Melbourne
while smaller capitals and regional areas continue to see much
milder downturns. Across price tiers, all segments are now

showing similar rates of decline after top price tiers had seen
a more pronounced and earlier downturn. 'Top tier' segments
have tended to lead recent price cycles.
Looking across jurisdictions,
• Sydney prices dropped 1.6% in October having averaged
similar‑sized declines over the previous five months. While
all segments and tiers continue to record falls, the last few
months has seen the pace of decline moderate a touch in the
top tier segments that have led cycles in the past, although

they are still a long way from stabilisation.
• Melbourne prices declined 0.8%mth, in line with
September's move and continuing the slower pace of
retreat. As noted, this likely reflects the weaker starting
point coming into the correction, prices already at 5½ year
lows. Interestingly, the detail shows the moderation in price
declines in recent months has been more pronounced for
'top tier' units where prices are back where they were in
2016.
Housing downturn deepens

forecasts are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially
from these forecasts.
3WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
• Brisbane prices are tracking a very fast rate of decline
that is also seeing large downward revisions. Prices fall
2% in October with September and August now both
showing 2.1% falls, revised down from 1.5% and 1.6%

respectively (August having previously been estimated at
just 1%, July's move having seen a similar series of revisions
–1.2% to –1.8%). The combined effect has taken the rolling
3mth change to –6.1%qtr from –4.7%qtr last months and
–2.7%qtr in August and –0.6%qtr in July. At 0.4%yr, annual
price growth has virtually stalled flat having run at 18%yr as
recently as May. All tiers and segments are recording similar
rates of decline.
• Perth is seeing something very similar, prices dropping a

further 1.7% in October, with September's decline revised
down to 2.1% from –1.2% and August's to 2.0% from –1.7%.
At 3.9%yr, annual growth is still positive but off steeply
from the 24% pace back in May. As with Brisbane, all tiers
and segments are recording similar rates of decline.
• Adelaide dwelling prices posted a somewhat milder 1.2%
decline in October, , September's –1.3% decline marked
down to –1.7%. Annual price growth slowed to 3.7%yr,

similar to Perth's but with less of a whiplash from strong
gains to large declines. Turnover has also been much
steadier, sales holding flat over the last three months and
down just 12.4%yr.
• The smaller capitals were again more mixed, price growth
still positive in Darwin (0.6%mth, 9.7%yr), relatively resilient
in Hobart (+0.5%mth, 5.5%yr) but weaker in Canberra
(–0.7%mth, –3.7%yr).
• Regional markets showed a 0.9% decline in Qld but slightly
milder declines in NSW (–0.7%) and Victoria (0.6%). Annual

prices growth is in the 1.4 ‑3.5%yr range.
All up, the October updates make for bleak reading. With the
RBA widely expected to raise interest rates again, the near
term outlook remains very challenging. Next week's consumer
sentiment update will show how this is shaping housing ‑
related sentiment – so far, consumer price expectations have
weakened but stopped short of swinging to the clear negative
consensus that emerged in the 2022 ‑23 correction. That now

looks to be at risk. Either way, momentum looks set to remain
weak into year‑end and, barring a significantly improved
inflation and interest rate outlook, well into 2027.

## Document 352: FX Outlook 2027_ Seven key risks

Source PDF: all report pdf/FX Outlook 2027_ Seven key risks.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Low volatility masks a wide range of tail risks for 2027. AI stress,
another inﬂation shock, ﬁscal concerns, elections and
geopolitical escalation could all trigger sharp FX moves, while
ﬁnancial repression and a carry unwind add further asymmetric
risks

Chris Turner and Francesco Pesole
AI bust: expect unorthodox FX reactions
The resilience of the AI trade in equities has been tested of late, and
a more decisive unwind could become an overarching FX driver next
year. We think the impact of a major US tech selloﬀ on currencies
would depend on how far it spreads to broader US and global risk
assets, whether it heavily threatens ﬁnancial stability, and how it
impacts the inﬂation backdrop. Crucially, the initial FX reaction may
look very diﬀerent from the medium-term move.
The dollar still retains decent safe-haven appeal by our estimates,
having recovered from the 2025 “sell America” period. Yet episodes
in 2026 where AI concerns hit tech stocks without materially

### Page 2

spilling into broader equities were accompanied by some USD
underperformance. We think contained US tech-speciﬁc weakness
would continue to weigh moderately on the dollar next year, with
low-yielding haven currencies such as the yen, Swiss franc and euro
beneﬁting the most.
The dynamics would be diﬀerent if tech losses triggered a broader
selloﬀ. In that scenario, we would expect an initial safe-haven boost
to the dollar, followed by a larger depreciation as the shock

deepens and the Federal Reserve response comes into focus. The
greater the threat to ﬁnancial stability, the stronger the case for the
Fed to provide liquidity and prevent a global ﬁnancial crisis-style
credit crunch, ultimately weighing on the dollar. Over the medium
term, we would expect JPY, CHF and EUR to outperform, while the
Australian dollar, New Zealand dollar and non-European EM
currencies would be hit harder, consistent with recent AI-driven

selloﬀs (see chart below).
There is, however, an important inﬂation caveat. A severe risk-oﬀ
shock would itself reduce inﬂation risks through potential job
losses, negative wealth eﬀects and tighter credit conditions. But if
inﬂation remains elevated, the Fed may be slower to ease, not least
to preserve the safe-haven role of the Treasury market. In that
scenario, we think the dollar would outperform for longer, while
European currencies would be less insulated than under a more

benign inﬂation backdrop.
Exposure to an AI bust among selected
currencies

### Page 3

Asian currencies have been excluded due to time-zone mismatch
Source: ING, Reﬁnitiv
A new 2022 for inﬂation
Our baseline assumes contained second-round eﬀects from the
energy shock, allowing major European central banks to cut rates
towards the end of 2027. Four risks could challenge that view.
Second-round eﬀects may simply take longer to emerge, as a
prolonged crisis and higher gas prices erode corporate buﬀers and
feed into food and services inﬂation. Tighter labour markets could

amplify the shock through stronger wage growth, while renewed
Middle East escalation could drive another sharp rise in energy
prices. Finally, greater ﬁscal support could cushion the hit to
growth, but at the cost of higher medium-term inﬂation pressures.
In such a scenario, we would expect broad dollar gains. Historically,
low-yielders have tended to suﬀer most when Fed tightening drives
global yields higher, but we would be wary of applying that

playbook mechanically in 2027. The relative central bank response
should be the key diﬀerentiator, with those that have tightened less
so far retaining more ammunition to defend their currencies.
We still see the Norwegian krone and Swedish krona as particularly
vulnerable given their low liquidity and high beta, while AUD could
be a standout loser given the Reserve Bank of Australia's limited
room to tighten further amid domestic vulnerabilities. EUR and JPY,

by contrast, could prove more resilient than in 2022, provided the

### Page 4

European Central Bank and Bank of Japan respond swiftly to

Current central bank rates vs latest peak
Source: ING, Macrobond
Fiscal takeover: contained, but
potentially explosive risk
We’ve all seen the French bond sell-oﬀ unfold over recent
months. The heart of the story is one of budget credibility. If
investors suspect that a government will not or cannot amend a
worrying budget trajectory, then they will demand higher

coupons or, in extremis, as in Greece 2010, refuse to buy
government debt. France is no Greece and our base case
assumes that some modest budget consolidation can keep
French spreads contained in 2027. Even if French politicians fail
to address investor concerns, the ECB’s Transmission Protection
Instrument (TPI) backstop can prevent broader contagion.
What about the US? One area of concern is that near 60% of US
Treasury issuance is in the form of short-term bill issuance, as

well as its large reﬁnancing requirements. What happens if
inﬂation spikes and the US Treasury has to pay ever-higher
reﬁnancing rates? Equally, what happens if White House policy
again upends the dollar-US Treasury safe-haven correlation as
was the case in March/April 2025? As we saw at the time, the
breakdown in these correlations can trigger some of the most

extreme dollar selling. And the fact that investors are again
running low FX hedge ratios on US assets warns of a similar
setup to early 2025.
Debt ﬁnancing overview
Source: IMF, ING
Watch the EU election calendar!
2027 will be a busy year for eurozone political risk, with markets
focused primarily on ﬁscal implications and the threat of EU
political fragmentation. Expect any negative EUR impact to make
the Swiss franc a standout outperformer, thanks to its European

safe-haven role.
French elections combine both ﬁscal and EU-wide risks and are
likely to matter most. The biggest downside for the euro would
come from a surprise victory by Jean-Luc Mélenchon in the April-
May presidential election or a lack of a clear majority in potential
parliamentary elections over the summer. To a lesser extent, a
Marine Le Pen victory could also weigh on sentiment if early signals
point to a more confrontational approach towards the EU than

already expected.
In Germany, several state elections will be watched for signs of
greater government instability or further gains by the Eurosceptic
Alternative for Germany (AfD). Markets will focus particularly on the
implications for Germany’s reform agenda, and by extension the
growth outlook.

### Page 6

Italian elections rank third among our political risks. Although
scheduled by December, a May-June vote looks increasingly likely.
Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni remains favoured to secure another
mandate, potentially backed by a broader coalition. Italian asset
resilience has relied on Meloni’s ﬁscal discipline, so a surprise
victory by the centre-left bloc would, in our view, pose material
downside risks to BTPs and the euro.
A busy election calendar for next year

Source: ING, average of pollsters
Debasement trade strikes back
2025 was the year of dollar debasement, when fears over a
captured Fed led to pressure on the dollar against the Swiss franc,
while bitcoin and gold went bid. We had a ﬂash of that trade again
this August, when Scott Bessent’s extension of longer-dated US
Treasury buybacks unnerved the bond market and USD/CHF was hit
hard.
Were US Treasury yields to rise sharply next year, the theme of

‘ﬁnancial repression’ could re-emerge. Here the US Treasury could
try to contain Treasury yields by providing regulatory incentives to
banks to hold more Treasuries or put pressure on the Fed to
intervene. Any suggestion that the Treasury would not be allowing

Treasury yields to fully compensate investors for inﬂation or ﬁscal
risk would see the dollar getting slammed.
USD is an efﬁcient safe-haven, but things
can change
Source: ING, Reﬁnitiv
The end of the carry trade?
Popular carry trade target currencies through 2026 have again
been the Turkish lira and the Nigerian naira. On the former, Turkish
presidential and parliamentary elections must be held by May
2028 and present some risk that the Central Bank of Turkey may

opt to change its policy of a controlled depreciation in the lira. That
would come as a big shock to the market. Nigeria looks slightly
better positioned after recent reforms to fuel subsidies and in the
energy sector.
When looking at the popular funding currencies of the yen, we
doubt slightly more aggressive BoJ tightening would be enough to
trigger a big short squeeze in the yen. And speculators are actually
running net long yen positions today as opposed to the large short

yen positions held in summer 2024 – the last time we had a major
adjustment in yen positioning.
The biggest driver of a carry trade unwind would probably come
through a ﬁnancial shock. Were any unwind in the AI trade to
uncover stresses or imbalances in, for example, the private credit
area and lead to ﬁnancial distress, volatility would surge and value-

### Page 8

at-risk considerations could prompt indiscriminate deleveraging.
We haven’t really seen much ﬁnancial distress since the Silicon
Valley Bank crisis of 2023.
A big carry trade unwind would be yen and Swiss franc positive,
while negative for the high-yielding activity currencies.
Geopolitical blowout: more than one
front to watch
There are three major fronts to watch in terms of geopolitical tail
risks next year. All of these would see the dollar as the big

outperformer.
A spillover of Russia’s war beyond Ukraine, or a material escalation
in cyber and hybrid attacks on European countries, would hit
European currencies the hardest. CEE currencies and the
Scandinavian bloc would be particularly exposed given their
geographical proximity and sensitivity to regional security risks.
The Swiss franc and yen would join the dollar in a big rally.
A broader Middle East conﬂict drawing in additional regional or

external powers would mainly hit FX through higher energy prices.
Net energy importers would suﬀer most, particularly currencies
where central banks have limited scope for additional tightening to
oﬀset the inﬂationary shock. Here, the dollar would be a clear
winner, as other low-yielders would suﬀer from the energy shock.
A sharp escalation in geopolitical tensions in East Asia would put
Asian currencies under heavy pressure, with AUD and NZD also

vulnerable as liquid proxies. European currencies would be relatively
more insulated from the initial shock. Regional risks could also
include US-China trade and technology tensions.
Are geopolitical risks the new normal?

## Document 384: Nebius Q3业绩超预期，资本开支升至270亿美元、ARR目标延期，股价跌近17% _ 财报见闻

Source PDF: all report pdf/Nebius Q3业绩超预期，资本开支升至270亿美元、ARR目标延期，股价跌近17% _ 财报见闻.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Nebius 三季度营收达 9.81 亿美元（同⽐增 571% ）、调整后 EBITDA 为 4.66 亿美元，双双超
出预期。然⽽，公司推迟并收窄了年化经常性收⼊（ ARR ）指引，称其不再适⽤，同时将
2026 年资本开⽀⼤幅上调⾄ 270 亿美元，并预计 2027 年进⼀步增⾄ 400 亿⾄ 450 亿美元。
股价暴跌 16.84% 。
摘要：
AI 云计算公司 Nebius Group 公布第三季度业绩，营收与利润双双超出市场预期，但 ARR 增⻓指
引下调与资本开⽀⼤幅上调的组合，令投资者对其盈利路径产⽣疑虑。
Nebius 三季度营收达 9.81 亿美元，较市场预期的 9.19 亿美元⾼出约 6.7% ；调整后 EBITDA 为
4.66 亿美元，亦⼤幅超出预期的 3.57 亿美元。然⽽，公司将年化经常性收⼊（ ARR ）⽬标从此
前承诺的 "2026 年 12 ⽉前达到 70 亿⾄ 90 亿美元 " 下调⾄ "2027 年 2 ⽉前达到 70 亿美元以上 " ，与此
同时，公司还表⽰ "ARR 不再是衡量业务的正确指标 " ，令外界对其战略⽅向的清晰度产⽣质

疑。
受财报表现不及预期拖累，公司股价在盘前交易时段即下挫逾 14% 。正式开盘后抛压持续释
放，股价⼤幅低开约 22% ，截⾄收盘跌幅虽有所收窄，但全天仍重挫 16.84% 。

## Document 434: Tracking Europe’s AI transition_ A new STOXX 600 earnings-call framework (2026-11-11)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Tracking Europe’s AI transition_ A new STOXX 600 earnings-call framework (2026-11-11).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-11-11
Pages: 16
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Tracking Europe’s AI transition:  
A new STOXX 600 earnings-call 
framework

1 
Authors 
Michael Kirker 
Economist

November 11, 2026 
How quickly is AI moving from experimentation into everyday business, and 
where are companies reporting tangible gains? The answers matter for 
institutional investors and policymakers assessing productivity, operating 
efficiency and labour demand. Yet comparable evidence across Europe remains 
limited, making it difficult to track the pace and scale of the transition. 1 
We address this gap with a proprietary framework that turns what is said on 
STOXX 600 earnings call into three structured indicators: AI adoption 
intensity, reported productivity benefits, and the impact on workforces. 
Large language models analyse what management teams say about AI’s 
operational role, applying a common scoring framework across companies, 
sectors and countries. Our new approach provides a timely pan-European 
comparable tracker that captures not just whether AI is being mentioned, but its 
real-world impact as the adoption of the new technology unfolds. 
Key Takeaways: 
— Adoption is broadening, with signs of faster progress. AI-use scores rose 
gradually from a low base before strengthening over the past 12 months. The 
increase extends across sectors, although the pace and level of adoption 
remain uneven. 
— Workforce impacts remain limited in corporate disclosures. The 
framework does not show a broad negative workforce signal attributable to 
AI. The aggregate score remains close to the level of no impact on the labour 
force across all of our sample. However, this does not rule out AI displacing 
workers at a future date. 
— Geographic differences persist within the listed-company sample. Since 
2022, German companies in the STOXX 600 have generally recorded above-
average AI-use and reported productivity scores. Dutch and Spanish 
companies in the STOXX 600 have tended to score lower on AI use, although 
Spain has recently gone through a rapid catch up. 
— The technology J-Curve: The findings are consistent with an early stage of 
the AI transition. Companies report increasing adoption of the new 
technology, but transformational productivity gains and extensive workforce 
restructuring remain uncommon.

1 1 Our US team regularly publish on data from the USCB showing the impact of 
AI on businesses, and our German team have recently begun their own 
chartbook. In the UK, the Office for National Statistics publishes on the topic too.

### Page 2

Deutsche Bank AG Page 2

Methodology 
Sample period: Our sample begins in January 2022, providing a baseline before 
ChatGPT’s launch on 30 November 2022. AI in this framework covers both 
established and emerging technologies, so the launch of ChatGPT is a reference 
point rather than the start of all corporate AI adoption. 
Timing of observations: STOXX 600 companies report on different financial 
calendars and at quarterly or semi -annual intervals. Their earnings calls also mix 
discussion of completed activity with forward-looking guidance. 
We assign each transcript to the calendar quarter in which the earnings call takes 
place, regardless of the financial reporting period discussed. The resulting 
indicators therefore track when information about AI use and its operational 
impacts becomes public rather than when implementation or the reported effect 
occurred. This provides a common dating convention across the sample while 
retaining differences in reporting frequency and disclosure timing.

Figure 1: Coverage of the STOXX 600 earnings calls

Source: Deutsche Bank Research. Notes: Share of the STOXX 600 is based on the current company weight in the index. Data collection for the current quarter (Q4 2026) is incomplete 
as some companies are still to report

Constructing the STOXX 600 AI corpus : We use a specialised AI transcription 
engine to process earnings calls from STOXX 600 companies, restricting the 
analysis to calls for which we obtain complete transcripts of sufficient accuracy. 
In Q3 2026, the latest full quarter, the dataset covered more than 500 companies, 
representing approximately 90% of the index’s current market capitalisation 
weights (Figure 1). In the current quarter (Q4) earnings calls are still taking place. 
Nearly 250 companies with an equivalent weight to around c.60% of the STOXX 
index having already reported. 
Historical coverage typically ranges from 300 to 500 companies and represents 
more than 80% of the index’s current market -capitalisation weights. Coverage 
peaks twice a year because some European companies report semi-annually.  
11 November 2026 
Focus Europe

### Page 3

Deutsche Bank AG Page 3

### Page 4

Deutsche Bank AG Page 4

For examples of the AI’s scoring and justifications, see the tables presented in the 
Annex at the end of this report. 
Summary statistics 
Figure 2 shows the distribution of scores across all earnings-call transcripts in the 
historical sample, without weighting for company size or reporting frequency. 
These are transcript-level distributions rather than shares of companies, and they 
combine early and recent observations. They provide context for the quarterly 
indicators: 
• AI use score : Approximately 60% of earnings call transcripts do not 
discuss AI use. Among the minority that do discuss it, most fall into the 
“Active implementation” range (a score of 4-6 out of 10). 
• AI’s productivity impact: Nearly 70% of earnings calls do not mention a 
tangible AI-related productivity benefit. Among calls that do, scores are 
generally in the medium -impact band, indicating notable operational 
improvements. Transformational gains, scored at 8 or above out of 10 , 
are rarely reported. 
• AI’s labour impact: Only around 10% of earnings calls discuss AI -related 
workforce adjustments. Reported changes typically receive scores of 1 –
2 in magnitude on the −5 to +5 scale, with a slight tilt towards hiring and 
upskilling rather than redundancies.

Figure 2: Raw distribution of scores across the entire sample

Source: Deutsche Bank Research. Note: Results are based on the raw number of transcripts, and are independent of firm size, and biased towards firms reporting more often.

Taken together, the distributions are consistent with an early adoption phase: 
companies discuss AI implementation more often than transformational 
productivity gains or extensive workforce restructuring. A technology J -curve 
offers one interpretation of this pattern,  with investment and organisational 
adaptation preceding broader benefits.  
This J-curve pattern is strongly corroborated by external micro-data, such as the 
Banco de España’s business activity survey  (EBAE) of Spanish firms, which 
11 November 2026 
Focus Europe

### Page 5

Deutsche Bank AG Page 5

similarly indicates that AI has a more immediate impact on upfront investment 
and productivity than on headcount. 
AI use: A broad-based rise, with signs of faster recent adoption 
The new AI -use indicator points to a broadening transition across STOXX 600 
companies, with early signs that adoption has accelerated recently. Scores rose 
only gradually from 2022  and 2024 , but the latest observations suggest a 
stronger pace across industries and country groups.  
The rise is broad -based rather than confined to one sector. Technology has led 
much of the sample, while financials show tentative recent outperformance and 
utilities have caught up from a low starting point. Among the large country groups, 
German companies have persistently recorded above-average scores.  
AI use across European industries 
To trace sectoral dispersion,  we group companies by STOXX industry 
classification and calculate a weighted average of their scores for each calendar 
quarter, using their latest STOXX 600 index weights. 2  
We focus on four sectors spanning different business models: financials, the 
largest services sector; industrials, the largest physical-assets sector; technology, 
a likely early adopter; and utilities, an infrastructure-heavy sector where adoption 
may proceed more slowly.  
Figure 3 presents the AI use score for these four key industries alongside the 
range for all industry scores (represented by the shaded area). 
• The AI use score is general ly low over time but is gradually rising from 
around 1 point in 2022 to around 3 in 2026. 
• AI-use scores are rising across industries, but differences between 
sectors are widening. The common direction therefore points to 
broadening adoption rather than uniform progress: sectors can 
participate in the transition while remaining at different levels of use. 
In terms of the industries of interest: 
• Technology’s early lead has narrowed.  The sector’s AI -use score was 
near the top of the industry range through to 2024  but has recently 
plateaued as the broader STOXX 600 average caught up during 2025 –
26.3 
• Financials and industrials broadly track the index average. Both sectors 
have remained close to the aggregate AI -use score  over history, with 
financials generally a little below average, and industrials a little above. 
But financials show tentative signs of moving above average in recent 
quarters.

2 The industries are: (1) Basic Materials , (2), Consumer Discretionary , (3), Consumer Staples , (4) Energy, (5) 
Financials, (6) Health Care, (7) Industrials, (9) Real Estate, (10) Technology, (11) Telecommunications, (12) 
Utilities. 
3 Q4 2025 saw a large increase in the AI use score for the “Technology” industry, jumping up to around 6. This 
one-off spike was driven by ASML’s announcement of a significant strategic partnership with and investment 
in Mistral AI ” to 'embed AI across our entire holistic portfolio in order to increase the performance and 
productivity of our systems and the yield of our customers' processes' . Because of ASML’s large market cap 
(around 49% of our Technology category), it distorts the weighted average significantly.

• Utilities have caught up from a low base.  After recording lower AI -use 
scores than other sectors for much of the sample, utilities converged 
towards the STOXX 600 average during 2026. The result shows that the 
rise in reported adoption extends beyond the sectors most closely 
associated with AI.

Figure 3: AI use score (0-10) by industry

Source: Deutsche Bank Research. Notes: The value for each industry is computed as a weighted average of all firms within the industry where the weights are derived from the current 
STOXX 600 share of each company. The markers denote the readings for the current incomplete quarter

As a cross -check, our findings align with the European Commission’s October 
Eurobarometer Flash survey of MSEs and Mid -Caps and the Eurostat annual ICT 
surveys, which show low overall intensity but a similar expansion in service - and 
tech-related sectors.  
AI use across countries 
Companies in the STOXX 600 tend to be large multi -nationals. That makes it 
difficult to pin down where physically the AI transformation is taking place within 
their organisation. Instead, our country comparison is done using two 
classifications of a company’s country: 
• Country of domicile: AI is a transformation technology, and this type of 
change is likely to be driven in organisation from the top down. We 
therefore consider a company’s country as that in which it is domiciled 
(which is likely to be where the headquarters is also). 
• Country of primary listing:  While companies can generally list in other 
countries, there tends to be a home bias in their choice of exchange.  
Figure 4 presents the AI use intensity metric by both of these measures of country 
(the company’s domicile and primary exchange listing ). Again, the shaded area 
shows the range across all classifications, with the largest countries/exchanges 
singled out. 
German-domiciled companies and the Xetra -listed (Frankfurt stock exchange) 
group record above-average AI-use scores in most quarters since 2022.  
Spain’s AI-use score has risen noticeably over recent quarters and now exceeds 
the aggregate average, reversing its earlier below-average position.  
11 November 2026
Focus Europe

### Page 7

Deutsche Bank AG Page 7

AI-use scores rise across country groups, with indications of faster growth from 
2025. The shared direction suggests a broad European listed -company trend, 
even though differences in score levels and timing persist between countries.

Figure 4: AI use intensity (0-10) by country

Source: Deutsche Bank Research. Notes: The markers denote the readings for the current incomplete quarter

As a geographical cross -check, our index matches the broad trends of the 
Eurostat ICT annual survey, which places Germany as a clear frontrunner among 
its large peers. But some differences do exist. For instance, Eurostat shows the 
Netherlands with a very high AI adoption.  As Figure 5 demonstrates, there is a 
positive correlation between the AI use score metric derived here, and that from 
Eurostat, even though the Eurostat data measures if firms are adoptin g AI, and 
our new use measure also incorporates the concept of how intensively firms are 
using AI.

AI productivity impact: Reported benefits are rising, but 
transformational gains remain uncommon 
Higher intensity of use does not necessarily mean firms are benefiting more from 
AI. But our AI productivity impac t metric does show a similar pattern to our AI 
usage metric, although it tends to be slightly lower, indicating that companies 
may not yet be reaping the full rewards for their AI investment. 
Financials stand out more clearly on reported productivity benefits than on AI-use 
intensity, while German companies again record relatively strong scores , but only 
in more recent history.

Figure 6: AI's impact on productivity (0-10) by industry

Source: Deutsche Bank Research. Notes: The markers denote the readings for the current incomplete quarter

AI productivity impact across European industries 
Reported AI -productivity benefits have become more prominent across 
industries. Figure 6 shows scores rising gradually during 2022 –25, followed by 
11 November 2026 
Focus Europe

### Page 9

Deutsche Bank AG Page 9

indications of a faster increase from late 2025. The profile resembles the AI -use 
trend in Figure 4, suggesting that the stronger adoption narrative is accompanied 
by more discussion of operational gains. 
The aggregate trajectories of the AI use and AI productivity impact scores are 
similar, but sector rankings differ. Technology’s early relative strength in AI use is 
not matched by its productivity score, while financials report above-average 
productivity benefits despite more modest use scores. 
AI productivity impact across countries 
Figure 7 plots the self -report impact of AI on company productivity by country. 
Between 2022 -23, German companies record ed average productivity impact, 
even though their AI use score was relatively high. However more recently, their 
AI impact score has persistently been above average.  The UK grouping shows 
persistent early outperformance in AI impact during 2022–23, followed by 
convergence towards the aggregate average.

Figure 7: AI's impact on productivity (0-10) by country

Source: Deutsche Bank Research. Notes: The markers denote the readings for the current incomplete quarter

AI labour force impact: On net, AI may be benefiting 
employment, for now… 
Across industries and countries, the recorded score for AI’s impact on the labour 
force remains relatively small. On average, the reported impact on companies ’ 
employment has been fairly neutral, with a marginal negative bias over the last 
year or so.

Figure 9: AI's impact on the labour force (-5 to +5) by industry

Source: Deutsche Bank Research. Notes: The markers denote the readings for the current incomplete quarter. Negative (positive) scores are associated with job losses (gains) 
associated with a company's AI use.

11 November 2026 
Focus Europe

### Page 11

Deutsche Bank AG Page 11

AI labour force impact across European industries 
Despite rising productivity, AI has not triggered the negative employment shock 
feared by the public. Aggregate labo ur impact scores across nearly all sectors 
hover near zero, with a slightly positive balance over history, and a slightly 
negative balance more recently in 2025-26 (Figure 9). But overall the magnitude 
of AI’s impact on labour remains low. For the STOXX 600 aggregate average, the 
score’s maximum deviation from 0 (no impact) is 0.43 on our 0-5 scale. 
AI labour force impact across countries 
Workforce-impact scores are also small across the main country groupings, with 
most countries slowing a slight negative tilt in recent years (Figure 10).

Figure 10: AI impact on the labour force (-5 to +5) by country

Source: Deutsche Bank Research. Notes: The markers denote the readings for the current incomplete quarter. Negative (positive) scores are associated with job losses (gains) 
associated with a company's AI use.

The lack of significant impact so far from AI on companies’ labour forces so far is 
consistent with results from our previous work looking at the other side of the 
labour market. Data from our dbDataInsights household survey showed that 
workers across Europe reported a fairly low  level of fear about losing their job to 
AI over the next year. The analysis in that report found results that were more 
consistent with workers adapting to the new AI world by upskilling and 
11 November 2026 
Focus Europe

### Page 12

Deutsche Bank AG Page 12

demanding higher wages. Those results would not be consistent with a world in 
which AI was actively replacing labour. 
These findings of a low impact on labour are highly consistent with recent 
academic research (e.g., Yotzov et al. , 2026), which found that nearly half of 
surveyed UK and German firms reported absolutely no impact on headcount from 
AI over the past three years, 
Conclusion 
The newly developed framework provides a recurring way to monitor company 
disclosures about AI’s impact on productivity and labour.  The central finding of 
this report is a broadening AI transition rather than clear evidence of an 
economy-wide productivity breakthrough. The new metrics indicate that AI use 
remains limited but is rising across the European countries and industries 
represented in the sample. Companies report low but growing productivity gains 
and little aggregate workforce impact so far. This pattern is con sistent with a 
technology J-curve, with firms still integrating the new technology, rather than 
proof that this trajectory will unfold. Limited reported workforce effects today do 
not rule out subsequent layoffs or wider organisational restructuring. 
Quarterly updates to this framework will track Europe’s AI transition and provide 
timely signals of whether companies are reporting more substantial productivity 
and employment effects. 
Beyond AI, the framework could be adapted to extract signals about employment 
decisions, investment decisions and the general economic outlook from earnings 
calls. These potential extensions could form the basis of alternative nowcasting 
models for important macroeconomic data; they are applications that will be 
explored in future work.

11 November 2026 
Focus Europe

## Document 452: What lessons can we learn to keep European industry competitive from the measures taken by Germany, as the cost of electricity continues to turn the screw_ (2026-10-22)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What lessons can we learn to keep European industry competitive from the measures taken by Germany, as the cost of electricity continues to turn the screw_ (2026-10-22).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
WHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM
THE MEASURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO
TURN THE SCREW?
Marianne Mueller and Pascal Devaux
issue 19
21 October 2026
The 2022 energy crisis clearly highlighted the issue of electricity prices in Europe — and, more broadly, the issue of energy prices. As
a result, European manufacturers’ competitiveness has been severely undermined when compared with the United States and China.

By reigniting tensions over electricity prices, the conflict that began in the Middle East in late February 2026 further underscores the
continent’s vulnerability to fossil fuel imports, despite the expansion of renewable energy. Faced with an environment of potential sharp
and sudden price rises, European governments do not have the same room for manoeuvre.
While the additional costs borne by European manufacturers are mainly linked to the share of gas in national electricity mixes, we will

first illustrate that, in Germany, grid operation and maintenance costs, as well as taxation, are also contributing significantly to these
costs.
We will then explore the solutions proposed by the German government under the new European measures regulating state aid to
electricity-intensive industries.
Finally, our estimates will show that, while these measures make Germany more competitive against some European countries, they
do not place it on an equal competitive footing with the United States or China. Nevertheless, Germany has made significant progress

in decarbonising and making its electricity mix more flexible from a European perspective. This should help to ensure that electricity costs
are reduced in the medium term.

worldWHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM THE MEA- SURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO TURN THE SCREW?
GAS: A KEY FACTOR IN THE ADDITIONAL ELECTRICITY COSTS
FACED BY EUROPEAN INDUSTRY
European manufacturers are still paying significantly higher elec -
tricity costs than their American and Chinese competitors. In 2019,

there was a 30% difference between the prices charged to European
electricity-intensive industries (corresponding to the lower end of the
industrial price range) and the average US industrial price. In 2025,
it stood at nearly 100% according to the IEA (2026) 1, after peaking at
an average of 148% between 2022 and 2023. There is currently a 50%
difference with their Chinese competitors2.
The disruption to Russian gas supplies since 2022 is behind the rising

gas prices, particularly as the EU has had to turn to liquefied natural
gas3 (LNG), which is more expensive than gas transported via pipe -
line and, above all, exposes Europe to global gas market conditions.
The growth of LNG has, in fact, intensified competition between the
major consumption regions, primarily Europe and Asia. European and
Asian prices now tend to move more in tandem (Chart 1). However, in
China, the prevalence of coal — a widely available local resource —

and a more regulated electricity price help to keep industrial prices
lower. At the same time, the United States enjoys plentiful domestic
gas, which is produced at a low cost, driven by the sharp rise in shale
oil and gas production over the past decade. By contrast, since 2022,
Europe has been more exposed to the cost of imported gas (90% of its
total consumption, according to Eurostat), while the United States and
China have enjoyed plentiful domestic resources.

Fluctuations and the level of gas prices are passed on to Eu -
ropean electricity prices via the ‘merit order’ marginal pricing
mechanism, with the wholesale price determined by the margi -
nal cost of the last (and most expensive) electricity source called
upon to meet demand.
As a result, gas plays a decisive role, with gas-fired power sta -
tions setting the price in around 40% of cases in Europe4, with si-
gnificant variations depending on national electricity mixes. It is

close to this average in Germany, below it in Spain (26%) and
France (17%), and above it in Italy (73%) 5 in 2025 6 7. Therefore,
electricity prices are still sensitive to fluctuations in gas prices,
even when electricity is not generated from gas (Chart 2). This
pass-through is particularly pronounced when demand rises
and requires the use of more expensive sources, or when supply
constraints arise (for example, during extreme heatwaves that
reduce wind-power generation or disrupt the cooling of nuclear

power plants).
1 Prices – Electricity 2026 – Analysis – IEA
2 The difference may be even larger for other manufacturers who do not receive the same support as electricity-intensive sectors.
3 Imports of Russian gas (via pipeline and LNG) accounted for around 45% of total European imports in 2021, compared with 14% in Q2 2026 (Bruegel, natural gas imports).
4 ACER, 2026, Key developments in EU electricity and gas markets, 2026 Monitoring report.
5 In Italy, in response to sharply rising gas prices in 2026, the government is currently negotiating a potential option of capping the prices of gas supplied to power stations in

order to limit the rise in the wholesale price of electricity. However, this can only be a temporary general mechanism, with the potentially major cost being borne by public funds
and/or households.
6 Eurelectric, 2026, Power Barometer.
7 EMBER, 2026, Latest energy shock reminds Europe of its risky reliance on gas.
8 Energy shock: 2026 vs. 2022 dashboard – 2nd October 2026 – Economic Studies – BNP Paribas
However, for the time being, the 2026 energy crisis is having a far

smaller effect on electricity prices than in 2022. On the one hand, the
2026 peak reached by the European TTF benchmark is still well below
that recorded in 20228.
In addition, there has been significant progress in decarbonising Eu -
ropean electricity mixes since 2022, which has helped to mitigate, on
average, the pass-through of gas prices to electricity prices.

SOME EUROPEAN COUNTRIES HAVE MANAGED TO STAY
COMPETITIVE, BUT GERMANY IS STILL THE EXCEPTION
Electricity prices partly vary due to countries’ electricity mixes
(Chart 3). Electricity-intensive manufacturers in countries where the
share of fossil energy is high face higher costs (Poland, Italy and Ire -
land) (Chart 4). Conversely, countries with a less carbon-intensive mix

(particularly France and Spain in recent times) are less directly ex -
posed to these fluctuations, with costs closer to the United States and
China (Chart 5).
The situation in Germany is paradoxical, as, despite progressively
greening its electricity mix and wholesale electricity prices (averaging
90 EUR/MWh over the 2023–2025 period) and average electricity costs
(across all industrial sectors) in line with the European average, the most
energy-intensive industries face higher costs than the European average

(and even more so compared with international competitors). This is
mainly due to grid costs and a tax burden above EU standards (Chart 6).
9 Eurostat data are used to compare prices with the rest of the EU. However, given that electricity prices are not uniform for businesses at a European level, there may be differences
between Eurostat and national sources. Jäger P ., 2025, “Cautionary tale or model for Europe? How member states should react to Germany’s new electricity subsidy”, Hertie School,

Jacques Delors Centre.
10 P . Devaux, 2026, “European Union: low carbon transition and energy sovereignty, a path fraught with obstacles”
In the first half of 2026, for the most electricity-intensive industries,
the difference compared with the European average was 9%, based on
the prices excluding taxes and levies provided by Eurostat. This gap is
mainly due to the cost of accessing the grid. If all taxes and levies are
included, the difference rises to 21% (Chart 7)9.

EUROPE’S APPROACH OF ADOPTING BOTH STRUCTURAL
PROGRAMMES AND TARGETED SUPPORT MEASURES
European programmes to support the low-carbon transition pursue
objectives relating to climate action (reducing greenhouse gas emis -
sions), sovereignty10 (increasing energy self-sufficiency) and competi -
tiveness (reducing the volatility and costs associated with fossil fuels).
The RePowerEU programme, which was launched in 2022 in response
to the energy crisis, primarily aims to reduce imports of Russian gas

and, more generally, to accelerate the low-carbon transition. Although
Europe has reduced its dependence on imports of Russian fossil fuels,
there has still not been enough progress around the energy transition
and cross-border connectivity. This is due to governance constraints

and struggles in implementing the programme in practice within each
Member State11.
State aid is still the main vehicle for this. In response to the 2021–
2022 energy crisis, the European Union temporarily relaxed the rules
on this type of aid in order to support businesses facing rising energy
prices. Any intervention of this kind, provided that it is selective and

could affect trade within the single market, must be authorised in ad -
vance by the European Commission in principle. Since June 2025, the
Clean Industrial Deal State Aid Framework (CISAF) has provided a more
sustainable framework for these kinds of interventions. In particular,
this framework, which runs until the end of 2030, authorises Member
States to grant temporary aid to electricity-intensive businesses to off-
set the cost of electricity. The aid must be targeted and come with

decarbonisation commitments12. Against the backdrop of the crisis in
the Middle East, the temporary METSAF scheme, adopted in April 2026,
helped (in relation to measures falling under the CISAF) to increase the
proportion of additional costs offset by governments from 50% to 70%,
thereby offering additional support to the most at-risk sectors.
GERMANY IS STILL SUFFERING FROM INSUFFICIENT IN-
VESTMENT IN ITS ELECTRICITY NETWORKS, WHICH THE
AID SCHEMES ARE ATTEMPTING TO OFFSET

The issue of electricity costs is a particularly hot issue in Germany,
given the importance of industry to the economy (around 20% of va -
lue added, one of the highest levels in Europe) and its exposure to
international competition. Furthermore, the delay in modernising
electricity infrastructure (only 22.6% of the high-voltage lines planned
for 2025 had actually been completed by that date) is causing bottle -
necks between the production areas in the north and the consump -

11 European Court of Auditors, 2026, “Implementation of the REPowerEU plan must be stepped up”.
12 It applies to the most electricity-intensive activities, which are seeing their competitiveness threatened by international competition, and follows the 4x50 principle: 50% of
electricity consumption is eligible for a subsidy; Of the eligible proportion, the subsidy may not exceed 50% of the wholesale price;
The final wholesale price paid by the company must not be less than €50/MWh;

50% of the aid received must be reinvested in decarbonisation (renewable energy generation, storage and improvements in energy efficiency); Around 90 sectors are covered by this
reform, accounting for approximately 2,000 companies, including in the chemical, steel and paper industries. The scheme, which is strictly regulated, will run for just three years
(from 1st January 2026 to the end of 2028) at an estimated total cost of between EUR 3 and 5 bn, financed by the special Climate and Transformation Fund.

13 Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2015) and Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2025). These costs mainly cover the costs of physical infrastructure; operational,
regulatory and compliance costs; and costs associated with using the transmission and distribution network.
14 The Industriestrompreis (industrial electricity price) is the public subsidy mechanism aiming to reduce the price of electricity for some industries.
15 The indirect carbon cost compensation mechanism is a government support measure for electricity-intensive industries, which aims to offset the cost of carbon-emission

allowances for energy producers.
tion centres in the south. As a result, the costs of accessing services
have increased fivefold over 10 years (rising from EUR 1 bn in 2014
to EUR 5.2 bn in 2024 13). At the same time, as we have already seen,
taxation and grid charges are hitting industry hard, with levies on large
consumers far exceeding the European average despite some support
mechanisms being put in place for the most electricity-intensive com-

panies (such as the Bandlastprivileg measure to reduce grid tariffs).
As a result, German industrial production has suffered in particular
due to rising energy prices in recent years, including electricity, with
electricity-intensive industries being especially affected (Chart 8).
In order to cope with these higher electricity costs than the European
average, Germany has been using European support schemes to reduce
the cost of electricity for businesses, particularly since the Merz go -

vernment took office in May 2025. The main scheme — the Industries-
trompreis14, made possible by the CISAF — has been approved by the
European Commission for the 2026–2028 period. While it is less bene-
ficial than the indirect carbon cost (ICC) offsetting included in electri -
city prices15, it has a broader scope, meaning that businesses ineligible
for the ICC can benefit from it. Other measures complement this targe-
ted support by addressing other components of the electricity bill. As a

result, the electricity tax has been set at the European minimum for
manufacturing sectors, while the gas-storage tax has been abolished.
The government is also subsidising grid fees, which should halve them.
Overall, according to our estimates based on 2024 prices, the final
price of electricity for these industries could fall by around a quarter
(to 116 EUR/MWh for industries with annual consumption of between
70 and 150 GWh, compared with a European average of 123 EUR/MWh)

(Chart 9). Germany’s advantage over Central and Eastern European
countries, where electricity costs are generally already higher, looks
set to increase.

However, even when taking all of the subsidies into account, the price
of electricity in Germany would remain higher than the levels seen in
France, Spain or Portugal (and even more so in the United States and
China).
FISCAL MEASURES: LIMITED ROOM FOR MANOEUVRE
There are a number of limitations to these European schemes. For
the country implementing them, lower electricity costs mean that

its industry becomes more competitive against industries from other
EU countries, but this does very little to address the disadvantage that
it faces compared with the United States and China. Therefore, this
measure will unlikely be enough to counteract the loss of market share
seen vis-à-vis the United States and China. In addition, the support
schemes are restrictive (conditional on investment in the low-carbon
transition) and are transitional. Finally, their cost is partly passed on

to the public purse or to consumers who do not receive this support
(in Germany, the cost of the new energy price support measures is
estimated at EUR 14.3 bn in 2026, or 0.3% of GDP16).
Some European manufacturers have proposed abolishing or reducing
the carbon tax as a solution to getting electricity prices down quickly.
In Germany, this tax accounted for 14% of the electricity price in the
first half of 2025, which is higher than the EU average (11%). However,

this proposal is already covered by the indirect carbon cost offsetting
mechanism for electricity-intensive industries exposed to carbon al -
lowance prices. In addition, while adjustments are being made — no -
tably to slow the rate at which allowances are reduced — it seems
highly unlikely that the scheme will be significantly challenged. It is
one of the cornerstones of Europe’s policy to combat climate change,
which has been effective thus far, as shown by the falling greenhouse

gas emissions from industry. More broadly, the gradual introduction of
a carbon border adjustment mechanism (CBAM) should mitigate com-
petition distortions caused by environmental practices17.
IN GERMANY AND THE REST OF EUROPE, HEAVY INVEST -
MENT IS DRIVING UP ELECTRICITY BILLS IN THE SHORT
TERM
In response to the challenge of the low-carbon transition and indus -
trial competitiveness, European governments must strike a balance
between decarbonising the electricity mix and ensuring a supply of

energy that is flexible enough (particularly from controllable sources)
to guarantee stable electricity systems. The integration of an increa -
sing proportion of intermittent renewable energy sources requires
massive investment in transmission and storage infrastructure (statio-
nary batteries). This expenditure, which places a long-term burden on
grid costs, reduces the scope for support schemes (particularly public
ones) to address competitiveness gaps. In Germany, the cost of the

transition is estimated at between EUR 122 bn, according to the go -
vernment (2.8% of GDP), and EUR 200 bn, according to the DIHK18 (4.6%
of GDP), by 2030–2035. If we take the ECB’s assumption19 that govern-
ments will cover around 17% of these investments, the annual public
16 The Industriestrompreis is estimated at around EUR 1.3 bn per year, the subsidy for network charges is projected to amount to EUR 6.5 bn in 2026, the abolition of the gas sur -

charge around EUR 3.4 bn, and the reduction in the electricity tax nearly EUR 3 bn per year. Furthermore, the ICC had cost EUR 2.3 bn in 2024 for costs incurred in 2023.

existing gas-fired power stations) and 2 GW potentially allocated to batteries (accounting for 80% of existing large-capacity stationary batteries intended for the wholesale market).

expenditure burden of the transition in Germany would be between
0.5 and 0.8 percentage points of GDP by 2030. However, this budgetary
pressure comes at the same time as three other budgetary increases:
rising social expenditure linked to an ageing population; the necessary
increase to defence budgets to meet NATO’s target of 3.5% of GDP by
2029; and the rising cost of debt-servicing20.
Germany has already made significant progress in maximising its use

of renewable energy, putting it at the head of the pack in Europe. The
country generates 20% of Europe’s renewable electricity (solar and
wind) and accounts for 30% of installed storage capacity (BESS) 21, far
ahead of other European countries (ranking third globally). However,
despite this momentum, Germany’s dependence on gas has not yet
decreased. As a matter of fact, it is only partially offsetting the planned
closure of coal-fired power stations and the phasing out of nuclear

reactors. Furthermore, the intermittent nature of solar and wind power
could prolong this dependence in the short and medium term. In order
to ensure that the German electricity system is flexible, new invest -
ments in gas-fired power plants, supported by public subsidies, are
planned22.

SUBSIDIES ARE HELPING TO ADDRESS THE EMERGENCY
CAUSED BY ENERGY CRISES WITHOUT RESTORING THE
INTERNATIONAL COMPETITIVENESS OF ENERGY-INTEN-
SIVE INDUSTRIES
In response to the impact of energy crises on industrial competitive -
ness, European governments are finding themselves having to strike a
delicate balance between providing short-term support to industries

exposed to increased international competition and continuing — if
not, accelerating — energy transition policies which will result in hi -
gher costs for electricity-network users for several years to come.
Beyond direct economic support measures, which are difficult to sus -
tain long term, four priority areas are emerging:
1/ Demonstrating flexibility in implementing European directives in
order to factor in national specificities and the differing timeframes

between energy crises (which can lead to irreversible industrial conse-
quences) and reforms (which have a longer-term horizon). The relaxa-
tion of the European carbon tax mechanism 23 appears to be a step
towards this.
2/ Continuing efforts to decarbonise the electricity mix, which are still
the main vehicle for sustainably reducing electricity prices for Euro -
pean industries in the medium and long term.
3/ Optimising the use of electricity networks on the demand side, in

particular through smart grids, a flagship element of the Commission’s
COM (2026) 600 reform presented last July24.
4/ Coordinating European electricity networks in order to harness the
complementarities between national systems.
Article completed on 19 October 2026

《地緣風險衝擊中東信用基本面 ，但緩衝能力仍佳 》
中東戰事持續外擴 ，除了美伊相互襲擊使得荷姆茲
海峽情勢依然緊張外 ，葉門胡塞武裝也對沙烏地阿
拉伯密集發動攻勢 ，柴油出口因此受阻 ，能源價格
處高位不墜 ，布蘭特原油 10月以來便維持在 100美
元/桶之上。過往中東國家往往是高油價的受惠者，
但受到能源生產和運輸中斷衝擊 ，國際貨幣基金10
月下調2026年中東及中亞地區經濟成長率預估值 1
個百分點至衰退，沙烏地阿拉伯更預估將衰退 3.3%。

新興當地貨幣債指數 6.80% 風險溢酬反應貨幣政策較差情境，升息預期淡化後殖利率下行
人民幣債指數 2.20% 景氣不均衡復甦，寬鬆貨幣政策為主要支撐力
亞洲當地貨幣債指數 4.60% 亞洲國家貨幣政策觀望，短線維持中性看法

重點摘要 沙烏地阿拉伯 (以下簡稱沙國 )雖極力避免直接捲入
戰爭，但仍遭受衝突外溢效應波及 。戰爭不僅導致
沙國大幅增加補貼 ，軍事和安全支出以及融資費用
上升均加大財政支出 ，因此，即便油價上漲抵消產
量下降的影響而使石油收入高於預期，沙國2Q26預
算赤字佔 GDP比重仍達 7.1%，遠高於年度目標的
3.3%。儘管近期公布的 2027年預算計畫 (PBS)目標
是將預算赤字佔 GDP比率從2026年的4.9%(沙國官
方最新預估 )縮小到2027年的3.6%，但若戰爭長時
間 延 續， 此 目 標 或 難 以 達 成， 目 前 估 計2027 年
17420億里亞爾的債務目標亦將輕易被超越 。不過
即使如此 ，沙國目前負債占 GDP比重僅約 35%，

2027年市場最悲觀預期也大約40%左右，仍是低於
全球新興國家均值約80%的水準。
中東另一主要發債國卡達對天然氣出口依賴度高 ，
天然氣出口受阻也使得國際機構普遍預估卡達 2026
年經濟衰退10%以上、幾乎是2010年以來最重，所
幸卡達2026年以前累積巨額經常帳盈餘及外匯儲備
作為緩衝，即使在今年3月外部環境面臨極大壓力下，
卡達仍有 199億里亞爾的經常帳盈餘 ，累積過去四
季經常帳占 GDP比重仍達 13.5%，現今外匯存底足
以彌補3月的貿易逆差，並可維持４年以上。這些數
據都顯示戰爭對中東國家的經濟及對外償債能力確
實已帶來短線壓力 ，但過去累積的龐大緩衝使其具
備良好因應能力 ，信用違約交換 (CDS)目前並未因
定價油氣供應長期中斷而大幅攀升 ，今年雖持續發
債但債務壓力並不高 ，IMF更預期2027年中東國家
經濟將成長6.3%。然中東國家對能源出口依賴度仍
高，能源出口是否長期受阻則是後續觀察重點。

不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資之風險，並就投
資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
重點摘要 《經濟強勁帶來的高利率無損新興債利差穩步收斂 》
財政收支並非驅動新興主權信用利差的唯一因素 ，
總體經濟環境 、市場流動性均牽動利差走勢 ，因此
雖然自7月以來沙烏地阿拉伯等中東國家信用利差又
再擴大，但也大概在3年均值附近並不算高。以歷史
經驗來看，BB級主權國家利差通常對財政惡化程度
反應較大 ，可能是因為該評等僅低於投資等級一級 ，
財政變化容易牽動投資人對升等至投資級的預期與
投資部位的調整 ，而在投資等級的主權國家中 ，則
以匈牙利和波蘭這些債務負擔較重的主權債利差較
易受財政問題影響。
油價和利率是近期2項重要觀察變數，也牽動利差走
勢，不過由於新興主權債指數中約5成的組成為大宗
商品出口國、3成為石油出口國，反而能受惠油價上

漲，油價上漲時與利差走勢敏感度也較油價下跌時
低；然而 ，更重要的是高油價對通膨和政策應對的
二階影響 ，將導致高利率長時間延續的可能性並最
終衝擊實體經濟 ，進一步限制石油出口國利差收窄 。
以長期趨勢來看 ，利率水準的絕對值高低不必然影
響新興債利差 ，利率走勢反而和信用利差呈現小幅
負相關，利率走升反映的若是強勁經濟成長而非能
源供給衝擊 ，此時往往能伴隨信用利差收窄 ，反而
需留意的是利率波動放大及利率的大幅重新定價 ，
這才是牽動利差波動加大的主因 。目前總體經濟環
境部分或可與2004~2007年相比擬，當時在強勁成
長的背景下利率長時間在4%~5.3%高位震盪，但新
興主要貨幣主權債利差仍可收斂至 134點(此波低點
189點)，若經濟成長能在現今高油價和高利率環境
下持續強勁，利差則可望保持穩定。
重點摘要
• BB級主權債及債務負擔沈重
的投資級主權債利差較容易
受財政問題干擾
• 新興主權債指數中約 5成為石
油出口國 ，降低油價上漲對
利差走勢的敏感性
3
即使油價上升，新興債利差與油價也少見正相關

資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
重點摘要 《景氣及企業獲利續成長 ，企業債信改善趨勢延續 》
三大信評機構持續認可新興市場四大區域企業信用
評等的改善，3Q26新興市場企業債淨升等規模大幅
提高至760億美元，其中獲上調規模達到910億美元、
創下2025年第四季以來的最高水平 ，也已是連續第
5個季度改善。
3Q26新興市場4大區域評等調升數量均高於下調 ，
其中以亞洲改善動能最為顯著 ，主要貢獻來自於亞
洲金融業 ，全年升評 /降評比率已來到 1.8倍，逆轉
2019年降評多於升評的劣勢；拉美企業仍是新興市
場中違約率最高的區域 ，降評債券數量與其他區域
比也確實較高 ，但降評企業家數已是過去 10年來最
低，加上阿根廷主權債信獲上調帶動企業債 (主要是
國有企業)信評調升，拉美航空業者如南美航空集團

(LATAM Airlines)在油價維持高位環境下仍於3Q26
獲信評機構調升至 BB+，在企業債信分化環境下仍
有亮點 ，因此第三季拉美企業淨評級上調規模達
370億美元，反而創下2025年第三季以來的最佳季
度表現 ，全年共有 234家拉美企業信評得調升 。新
歐中東地區債信今年算是少數債信惡化的區域 ，很
大部分是受到戰爭及高油價影響所致 ，不過奈及利
亞和南非等主權評級上調仍帶來正向效果 ，梯瓦製
藥(Teva)評等更是上調至投資等級，帶來了120億美
元的貢獻，部分抵消地緣政治風險帶來的影響。
強勁的獲利成長持續支持新興企業穩健基本面 ，有
限的違約率輔以韌性十足的總經環境 、以及活躍但
不過度集中的初級市場 ，均是支持債信改善的原因 ，
預期在企業獲利仍看好的情境下 ，第四季企業債信
仍將朝正向發展。

不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資之風險，並就投
資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
重點摘要 《聯準會升息年 ，新興債雖有波動但正報酬機率高 》
觀察過往聯準會升息年度 ，利率溫和走升機率相對
較高，新興債市雖然多會受到升息壓力而出現一波
跌勢，但全年度仍有較大機率收紅 ，1996年以來僅
有2018、2022這兩個升息年度新興債市全年收黑 。
不過2022年新興債市重挫很大一部分原因來自於聯
準會在短短一年時間內將基準利率自0％升至4.25%，
這種猛爆性升息與目前所預設的風險管理式的溫和
升息環境有所不同；另外 ，2018年聯準會雖只升息
四碼，但土耳其及阿根廷因美元稀缺引發流動性風
險，這2國經常帳赤字占 GDP比重當時均高於 5%，
對外融資壓力上升的同時進而爆發金融危機 。然而
現階段新興國家經常帳已普遍改善 ，土耳其經常帳

赤字已降至 2%~3%，阿根廷更轉為順差 ，基本面
與2018年有很大的差異。若扣除這兩段期間，新興
債市在聯準會升息期間波動雖可能放大 ，但在貨幣
政策方向更趨明朗後 ，走勢便會止穩反彈 ，全年平
均報酬率仍有10%以上。
至於新興市場企業債部分 ，過去因升息所造成的跌
勢約是主權債的一半 ，主要是因為企業債發行天期
多較主權要來得短 ，加上該期間獲利動能強勁 ，因
此在利率上升時期表現往往相對主權債更具韌性 。
儘管利率走勢仍存不確定性，但最終若能穩定在5%
左右，預估新興企業債全年總報酬仍可維持在3%，
除非未來出現更大的外部衝擊 ，如利率持續大幅上
升引發對經濟成長受損的擔憂 ，或AI驅動的成長模
式被重新評價 ，否則對新興債市的看法仍將維持正
向，新興主要貨幣主權債在這樣的大環境下 ，短線
雖相對承壓，但後續反彈力道也可望大於企業債。
• 新興主要貨幣主權債在聯準
會升息年度正報酬機率近8成
• 新興企業債相對抗跌 ，公債
利率若穩定在5%左右，全年
總報酬仍可維持在3%
5

## Document 515: バーナム新政権も財政拡張路線を堅持

Source PDF: all report pdf/バーナム新政権も財政拡張路線を堅持.pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 6 日

MURC Focus

バーナム新政権も財政拡張路線を堅持

～10 月末発表の秋季予算案で具体的な財源を示せるか～

調査部 主任研究員 土田 陽介

1. 伝統的な財政拡張を訴えるバーナム新政権 
英国では、9 月 27 日から 30 日にかけて、北西部の主要都市リバプールで与党である労働党の党大会が開催
された。7 月 20 日に就任したアンディ・バーナム首相にとって初の党大会となると同時に、新政権の財政運営方
針が示されるという点で、この党大会は非常に高い注目を浴びた。この場で示されたのは、中道左派である労働
党らしい、伝統的な財政拡張路線に基づくプランだった。

具体的には、バーナム 新政権の関係閣僚らは、政府調達（政府が民間からモノやサービスを購入すること）の
増強による景気浮揚策を主張した。例えばジョン・ヒーリー財務相は 28 日の党大会で、スコットランドのクライド海
軍基地に浮きドック 3 基を建設する他、海洋調査船を建造することに数十億ポンドを投資する計画を発表した。
いずれも国内の業者に発注することで雇用や所得につなげる方針である。

また労働党大会に先立つ 26 日、バーナム新政権は、新築住宅を初めて購入する国民向けに新たな住宅購
入支援策を導入すると発表した。保守党政権下の 2013 年から 2023 年にかけて実施された“Help to Buy”と似
たスキームであり、購入者は購入価格の 2.5%に相当する頭金を支払えば、政府が購入価格の 20%までを融資
するというものである。

このように、バーナム新政権が目指す方針は、労働党に伝統的な“大きな政府”による財政拡張路線を反映し
ている。そうした点で、バーナム新政権の方針はキア・スターマー前政権とはあまり変化がない。一方で、新政権
が示した財政拡張路線が金融市場の評価を得るためには、財源を明示する必要がある。10 月 28 日に発表する
秋季予算案で、金融市場が納得する内容を示せるかが鍵を握る。

〇9月末にリバプールで開催された労働党の党大会で、バーナム新政権の財政運営方針が示されたが 、それ
は中道左派である労働党らしい伝統的な財政拡張路線に基づくプランだった。 
〇一方、金融市場 では、それまではバーナム 人気によって労働党 の政権運営が安定するとの思惑から“英国
買い”が先行していたが、党大会前後から調整色を濃くしている。 
〇増税は有権者の反発を、国債発行が投資家の反発を生むと予想される中、秋季予算で財政拡張の財源の
筋道をきちんと示せなければ、バーナム新政権も短命で終わりかねない。

### Page 2

2. 財政拡張を続けてきた近年の英国 
バーナム新政権は財政拡張に活路を 見いだそうとしているが、これまでも英国の政権は財政拡張に臨んでき
た（図表 1）。例えば英国の実質 GDP（国内総生産）に占める公需（＝政府支出＋公的固定資本形成）は、保守
党政権（テリーザ・メイ元首相）期の 2018 年後半に 21.5%を下回るまで低下したが、以降は上昇し、直近 2026
年 4～6 月時点で 24.4%となっている（図表 1）。

この間、2020 年 1～3 月期に生じたコロナショック後の公需の減退も限定的にとどまっており、結局は公需が
GDP に占める割合は“V 字回復”することとなった。労働党が政権を奪取したのは 2024 年 7 月の総選挙だが、
それ以前の保守党政権下でも、英国は事実上、財政拡張を続けていたことになる。その結果、公的債務残高の
対 GDP 比率も、一時は高インフレのために低下したが、再び上昇した（図表 2）。

このように近年の英国は、保革の立場を問わず財政拡張を続けてきた。しかし、EU 離脱やエネルギー不安に
伴う供給制約の高まりの中でそうした手法を取り続けたため 、英国はスタグフレーション（景気停滞と物価高進の
併存）に突入した。その改善のために必要な処方箋は、規制緩和を通じた供給の刺激である。しかし、それは“小
さな政府”を追求することにもなるため、中道左派として“大きな政府”を目指す労働党は容認できない。

バーナム新首相は地方の主導の経済成長を訴え、そうした意味での分権化に意欲的と考えられる。 ただし労
働党としては、公需による民需の刺激を通じた景気浮揚を引き続き志向していることが、秋の党大会で示された
形である。一方、金融市場では、バーナム新首相就任直後は、首相の人気に支えられて新政権の運営が安定す
るとの思惑から“英国買い”が先行していたが、党大会前後から調整色を濃くしている。

### Page 3

3. 財源なき財政拡張で信用を失うリスク 
スターマー前政権もまた、財政拡張路線による景気浮揚を目指した が、同時にそれは、供給の底上げを狙うも
のでもあった。老朽インフラ（鉄道や水道など）の再開発はその最たるものである。またスターマー前政権は、財政
規律にも配慮し、財源を国債発行よりも増税でカバーし、 財政拡張策の整合性を取ろう とした。財源を示したこと
を投資家は評価したが、増税に有権者が反発したため、結局、スターマー前政権は短命に終わった。

スターマー前政権下で低迷した労働党の支持率は、バーナム人気に支えられ“V 字回復”し、右派の改革党
（UK Reform ）と肉薄するに至る（図表 3）。労働党としては打ち出した目玉政策を着実に遂行し、有権者の支持
をさらに確たるものにしたいところである。とはいえ、そうした目玉政策は財政拡張を伴うものであるため、財源をど
う確保するか明確な筋道を示さなければ、金融市場の動揺を誘う。

すでに英国の長期金利（10 年国債流通利回り）は、グローバルなインフレ加速懸念に伴う世界的な金利上昇
の波に飲まれ、上昇が続いている（図表 4）。金融市場は財政拡張に対して神経を尖らせているが、秋季予算で
明確な財源が示さなければ、それを嫌気した投資家が英国債（ギルト債）の売却を進め、金利が一段と上昇し、
場合によってポンド安と株安の“トリプル安”を引き起こす可能性もある。

財政拡張の財源を増税で賄おうとすると、スターマー前政権のように有権者の支持を失いかねない。 一方、国
債発行で賄おうとすると、長期金利の押し上げ圧力になる。ゆえに、歳出の見直しで財源を賄おうとするだろうが、
簡単ではないため、具体的な筋道を示せなければ、結局は金利の上昇につながる。秋季予算で財政拡張の財
源の筋道をきちんと示せなければ、バーナム新政権も短命で終わりかねない。

## Document 523: ベッセント長官が日本の金融・財政政策を気にする理由

Source PDF: all report pdf/ベッセント長官が日本の金融・財政政策を気にする理由.pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 20 日

調査レポート

ベッセント長官が日本の金融・財政政策を気にする理由

～米国の国債市場が抱える潜在リスクと日本への示唆

調査部 研究主幹 中塚伸幸

〇米国のベッセント財務長官が日本の金融･財政政策に「注文」をつける背景には、日本の政策動向が米国の
国債市場に悪影響を及ぼしかねないとの懸念がある。 
〇そもそも米国の連邦政府の赤字は GDP の 6%程度で高止まりしており、フローの観点だけをとれば日本より
状況は厳しい。特に、利払費が歳出の約 14%を占め、大きな負担になっている。財政赤字に加えて、米国債
市場には調達の短期化、ヘッジファンドの保有拡大、民間起債との競合といったリスク要因もある。 
〇日本もいずれ利払費の増加に直面することが見込まれ、ベッセントの「正論」は傾聴に値するといえよう。

1. ベッセント長官は米国債市場への影響を懸念し、日本の政策に「注文」 
米国のベッセント財務長官は 9 月の G20 財務相・中央銀行総裁会議後の会見で「日本はリフレ政策をやめる
べきだ」と発言した。また、これに先立つ日銀･植田総裁との会談では「円安に対処するための断固とした市場･金
融政策を支持する」と伝えた、と米･財務省が公表している。さらに、ニューヨークタイムズ紙は、ベッセント長官が
5 月の片山財務大臣との会食の場で「日銀が利上げすれば円安を是正できる」と大臣に伝えたと報じている。 
米国政府が日本の経済政策に言及・干渉することは珍しいことではないが、ベッセント長官は日本の金融･財
政政策に「注文」をつけている。これは、「米国債のトップセールスマン」を自認する財務長官が、日本の政策動向
が米国債市場に悪影響を及ぼすことを強く懸念しているためと考えられる。それだけ米国の国債市場は不安定
化リスクを内包しているということであり、実際、10 年債の金利は年初の 4.2%程度から足元は 5.2%程度まで上昇
している。日本が円安是正のために米国債を売却するといったことは避けてもらいたく、日本はまずは自らの金
融・財政政策によって円安に対処せよ、ということであろう。 
ベッセントが日本の政策を気にする背景にある、米国債市場のリスク要因について、以下に整理する。 
2. 米国の連邦政府赤字は GDP 比 6%、日本よりも状況は厳しい 
米国の財政状況は厳しい。今月公表された CBO の速報値では、2026 会計年度（25 年 10 月～26 年 9 月、
以下、「年度」）の連邦政府赤字は約 2 兆ドル（1 ドル＝158 円換算で 316 兆円）と、前年比で 12%増加した（次
ページ図表 1）。名目 GDP に対する比率では、26 年度を含めここ数年 6%程度の高水準が続いている。歳出は
パンデミック対応で 20 年度と 21 年度に急増したあと、22 年度以降は縮小したが、24 年度以降は再び増加が続
く。歳入も増加してはいるが、歳出増には及ばず、赤字が拡大している（図表 2）。

### Page 2

歳入･歳出の定義が異なるため日米の比較は難しいが、米国に近づけるために日本の一般会計の歳入から公
債金と前年度剰余金受入を控除し、歳出から債務償還金を控除して収支尻を試算すると、24 年度決算ベースで
日本の中央政府（一般会計）の赤字幅はGDP の 3%程度とみられ、かつ、足もとで比率は改善傾向にある。米国
は GDP の 6%という高水準の赤字が続き、改善の兆しも見られず、日本以上に厳しい状況といえるだろう。

日米の中央政府の歳入・歳出構造の違いとしては、米･連邦政府の収支には日本では社会保障基金に相当
する収支が含まれる点もある。具体的には、歳入にはペイロール税（日本の社会保険料に相当）が含まれ、歳出
にはソーシャル･セキュリティ･ベネフィット（老齢年金給付等）、メディケア（高齢者医療保険給付等）などが含まれ
る。したがって、より平仄を合わせる形で比較するには、中央政府に地方政府と社会保障基金も加えた「一般政
府」ベースで日米を比較することが適切である。この点、IMF の直近の World Economic Outlook Database に
基づいて一般政府ベースの財政収支とグロスの政府債務を比較すると（図表3、4）、政府債務の GDP 比率は日
本が圧倒的に高い一方で、GDP 比でみた財政赤字は米国のほうが日本より厳しい状況にあるといえる。

### Page 3

3. 米国は利払い負担が極めて大きい  
財政収支の悪化は主要先進国に共通する傾向であるが、特に米国の状況が厳しい理由として、利払費負担
が非常に大きいことがある。22 年以降の長期金利上昇に伴って利払費は急増しており、25 年度の利払費（Net 
interest）は歳出全体の 14%を占め、国防費よりもウエイトが高くなっている（図表 5）。利払費は 22 年度から 24
年度まで 3 年連続して前年比 30%以上の伸びであり、25 年度も 10%増、26 年度は若干定義が異なる Net 
interest on public debt の速報ベースで 11%増と、依然として高い伸びが続く。 
 日本の一般会計の利払費（決算ベース）は 24 年度まで 7～8 兆円でほとんど横ばいであるのに対し、米･連邦
政府歳出の利払費は急激に増加しており、大きな違いがある（図表6）。これは、日本の場合は超低金利が長く続
き、その間に調達した債券のウエイトが高いため、債券残高で加重平均した実効金利が低い（ 25 年度末の加重
平均金利は 0.98%）のに対し、米国では金利水準自体が日本より高いうえに、22 年以降急激に長期金利が上昇
したことが影響している（米国債の加重平均金利は 26 年 9 月末で約 3.5%）。 
 ただし、日本の場合も、 10 年債の市場金利はすでに 3%程度まで上昇しており、今後、これに伴う利払費の増
加は避けられない。24 年度までの決算ベースでは図表 6 のようにほぼ横ばいであるが、当初予算ベースでは利
払費は 24 年度の 9.7 兆円から 26 年度には 13.0 兆円へと 2 年間で 3 割以上増加しており、借り換えの進展に
伴ってさらに増加していくものと見込まれる。

### Page 4

4. 法人減税が歳入の伸びを抑制  
 米･連邦政府の歳入は相応に増加している。特に、歳入全体の半分を占める個人所得税は 25 年度に前年比
9%増、26 年度も同 7%増と、着実に伸びている。一方、法人所得税は、歳入全体に占めるウエイトは1 割以下で
あるが、トランプ政権による減税（OBBB 法による一部設備投資の即時償却の恒久化等）により、26 年度は前年
比で 16%も減少している（25 年度も、24 年度には災害等による前年の延期納付分があったことを調整した上で
も、やはり減少）。即時償却によって投資が促進され、将来的に税収増となる可能性はあるが、少なくとも足元はト
ランプ政権の減税政策が歳入を抑えている面は否めない（図表 7、8）。なお、関税も、相互関税が違法とされ還
付となったことから、26 年度は前年比 11%の減少となった。

5. 米国債市場の潜在リスク ～期間の短期化とヘッジファンドによる保有の拡大  
 上述の財政収支そのものの悪化に加えて、米国の国債市場には潜在リスクとなる構造的特徴がある。一つは、
調達が相対的に短期化しており、借り換え対応のプレッシャーが相応に高まっていることである。米国債の平均
残存期間は 5 年 11 カ月と日本の普通国債の平均残存期間 9 年 5 カ月に比べて短い。また、米国債のうち 1 年
以内に償還される債券の比率は 26 年 9 月時点で 33%と、19 年の 27%から上昇しており（次ページ図表 9）、日
本の普通国債の 13%と比べても比率は高い。IMF も、こうした調達短期化の潜在的なリスクを今年 4 月の 4 条
報告で指摘している1。 
 もう一つはヘッジファンドによる保有が拡大していることで、状況によってはそれが市場を不安定化させる懸念
があると指摘されている。FRB は今年 6 月の論文で、国債全体のうちヘッジファンドが保有する比率は 23 年の
約 4.5%から 25 年 9 月には約 8.5%に拡大しており、ヘッジファンドはレバレッジをかけた裁定取引が多いため、
何らかのショックが起きた場合に市場の混乱を増幅させる可能性がある、と警鐘を鳴らしている2。

1 IMF, “United States: 2026 Article IV Consultation,” April 2026 
2 Phillip J. Monin, “Decomposing Hedge Funds’ U.S. Treasury Exposures,” FEDS Notes, June 22, 2026

### Page 5

6. 民間の投資適格社債との競合  
 米国債市場に影響を与える要因としては、民間の投資適格債の起債が増加していることも挙げられる。ハイパ
ースケーラーによるデータセンター建設資金の調達などにより、投資適格社債の発行額は 24 年以降に急増して
おり、26 年も第 3 四半期までの累計額で前年比 25%増と、すでに 25 年通年の実績と同水準に届いている（図
表 10）。こうした投資適格債は比較的安全な資産として米国債と競合するため、国債の需給を緩ませる要因にな
り得る。実際、FRB のウォーシュ議長は 9 月 FOMC 後の会見で、長期金利上昇の要因として、強い景気、地政
学的要因とともに、「資金調達の競争（competition for capital）」を挙げている。

7. 日本への示唆 ～「正論」は傾聴に値する  
 ベッセント長官の「注文」は、内政干渉ともいえるが、しかし正論である。背景にある米国の状況を整理したが、
米国側の事情を抜きにしても、日銀の利上げと財政規律の維持は円安是正の点から日本に必要なことと思われ
る。特に、財政運営においては、足もとはインフレによる税収増に加えて、過去の低利調達の恩恵に負う部分が
大きく、現状約 1%の実効金利はいずれ 3%程度に上昇する。そうなれば、利払費は今よりも 20 兆円膨らむ。さら
に、防衛費や社会保障費の拡大などが見込まれる中、来年度からの食品消費税減税の財源も明らかになってい
ない。ベッセント長官の「助言」は真摯に受け止めるべきであろう。

---
## Document 605: 凱基投顧_5347 世界先進_潘俊宏_20261106
